, we let
G_{i}=G[E_{i}]
. For all
0\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log L\right\rceil
, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

1.3
to graph
G_{i}
, with parameters
k
and
\delta
that remain unchanged, and parameter
\Delta=\left\lceil n^{20/k}\right\rceil
. We denote the graph
H
that the algorithm maintains by
H_{i}
. Recall that
|E(H_{i})|\leq n^{1+O(1/k)}\cdot\Delta\leq n^{1+O(1/k)}
. From
Section

9.1
, it is immediate to verify that
H_{i}
is a
k^{\prime}
-spanner for
G_{i}
, for
k^{\prime}=k^{19}\cdot 2^{O(1/\delta^{6})}
. Finally, we let
H=\bigcup_{i}H_{i}
. Notice that
|E(H)|\leq O\left(n^{1+O(1/k)}\log L\right)
must hold, and it is easy to verify that the algorithm from
Theorem

1.3
can be extended to maintain the graph
H
with worst-case update time
n^{O(\delta)}
, such that the number of edge updates that
H
undegroes following each update to
H
is at most
n^{O(1/k)}
.

Low-Congestion Spanners.

We formalize the notion of
low-congestion spanners
, that was implicitly introduced by Chen et al.
[
21
]
. The new notion views a spanner
H
of
G
as a sparse subgraph of
G
, with the additional property that
G
can be embedded into
H
with low congestion and path length bounds.
Notice that, if we use the standard notion of a
t
-spanner
H
of
G
, then
G
can be embedded into
H
via paths of length at most
t
, but the congestion of the embedding may be as large as
|E(G)|
. Low-congestion spanners aim at optimizing the length and the congestion parameters of this embedding simultaneously. We now provide a formal definition of low-congestion spanners.

Definition 9.3
(Low-Congestion Spanners).

Let
G
be a graph, let
H\subseteq G
be a subgraph of
G
with
V(H)=V(G)
, and let
d,\eta>0
be parameters. We say that
H
is a
(d,\eta)
-low congestion spanner for
G
, if there is an embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}=\left\{Q(e)\mid e\in E(G)\right\}
of
G
into
H
, such that the length of every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}
is a most
d
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
cause congestion at most
\eta
.

We note that low-congestion spanners can also be defined with respect to vertex congestion, and it is the spanners of the latter kind that were considered by
[
77
]
. Their algorithm maintains such a spanner
H
for a dynamic
n
-vertex graph
G
, with
|E(H)|\leq O(n^{1+o(1)})
, together with the embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
G
into
H
, such that the lengths of the embedding paths are bounded by
d=n^{o(1)}
, and vertex-congestion of the embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
is bounded by
\eta=n^{o(1)}\cdot\max_{v\in V(G)}\left\{\deg_{G}(v)\right\}
. The amortized update time and recourse bounds are
n^{o(1)}
.

We use the following simple lemma.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph, and let
\mathcal{R}=\left(\mathcal{C},\{C^{\prime}\}_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\right)
be a router decomposition of
G
with parameters
\Delta^{*},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta}
and
\rho
. Then for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, there is an embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}
of
C
into
C^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
\tilde{d}
, that cause congestion at most
\eta=\max\left\{\frac{16\tilde{\eta}\cdot\Delta_{\max}(C)}{\Delta^{*}},16\log n\right\}
, where
\Delta_{\max}(C)
is the maximum vertex degree in
C
.

Proof.

Consider a cluster
C\in\mathcal{C}
, and recall that
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
is a subgraph of
C
with
V(C^{\prime})=V(C)
, that is a
(\overline{\Delta}^{*},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
-router for the set
V(C)
of supported vertices.

We define a demand
{\mathcal{D}}=\left(\Pi,\left\{D(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi}\right)
as follows: set
\Pi
contains all pairs
(u,v)
of vertices, for which edge
(u,v)
lies in
C
, and the corresponding demand value
D(u,v)
is
1
.
Clearly, demand
{\mathcal{D}}
is
\Delta_{\max}(C)
-restricted in
C^{\prime}
. It is now enough to show that demand
{\mathcal{D}}
can be routed integrally in
C^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
\tilde{d}
, that cause congestion at most
\eta
.

Consider the demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
that is obtained from
{\mathcal{D}}
by scaling every demand
D(u,v)
down by factor
\frac{\Delta_{\max}(C)}{\Delta^{*}}
. Then it is immediate to see that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
is a
\Delta^{*}
-restricted demand, and so there is a routing
f
of this demand in
C^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
\tilde{d}
, that cause congestion at most
\tilde{\eta}
. By scaling the flow on each flow-path up by factor
\frac{\Delta_{\max}(C)}{\Delta^{*}}
, we obtain a routing
f
of the demand
{\mathcal{D}}
in
C
via paths of length at most
\tilde{d}
, that cause congestion at most
\frac{\tilde{\eta}\cdot\Delta_{\max}(C)}{\Delta^{*}}
. Our last step is to turn the resulting routing into an integral one, using the standard Randomized Rounding procedure of Raghavan and Thompson
[
71
]
.

Consider any edge
e=(u,v)\in E(C)
, and let
{\mathcal{P}}_{u,v}
be the set of all
u
-
v
paths in
C^{\prime}
of length at most
\tilde{d}
. Since flow
f
sends
1
flow unit from
u
to
v
over paths of length at most
\tilde{d}
, it defines a probability distribution
D_{u,v}
over the paths of
{\mathcal{P}}_{u,v}
, where every path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}_{u,v}
is assigned probability
f(P)
. For each such edge
e=(u,v)\in E(C)
, we choose a single path
P(e)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{u,v}
from this distribution, where the choices between the different edges are independent. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}=\left\{P(e)\mid e\in E(C)\right\}
be the resulting collection of paths, that defines an embedding of
C
into
C^{\prime}
. Clearly, the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
have length at most
\tilde{d}
each. Next, we argue that with high probability, the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
cause congestion at most
\eta
in
C^{\prime}
. Indeed, the expected congestion for an edge
e\in E(C^{\prime})
is at most
\frac{\tilde{\eta}\cdot\Delta_{\max}(C)}{\Delta^{*}}
. Using the Chernoff bound from
Section

2.9
with
t=\eta=\max\left\{\frac{16\tilde{\eta}\cdot\Delta_{\max}(C)}{\Delta^{*}},16\log n\right\}
,
we get that the probability that the congestion on
e
is greater than
\eta
is bounded by
2^{-\eta}<1/n^{3}
, since
\eta\geq 16\log n
. Using the union bound, with high probability, the congestion on every edge of
C^{\prime}
is at most
\eta
.

We obtain the following immediate corollary of
Section

9.1
.

Corollary 9.5
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph, and let
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{C},\{C^{\prime}\}_{C\in\mathcal{C}})
be a router decomposition of
G
with parameters
\Delta^{*},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta}
and
\rho
. Consider the corresponding graph
H^{\prime}
whose vertex set is
V(G)
, and edge set is
E^{\operatorname{del}}\cup\left(\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}E(C^{\prime})\right)
, where
E^{\operatorname{del}}=E(G)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}E(C)\right)
.
Then graph
H^{\prime}
is an
(\tilde{d},\eta)
-low congestion spanner for
G
, where
\eta=\max\left\{\frac{16\tilde{\eta}\cdot\Delta_{\max}(G)}{\Delta^{*}},16\log n\right\}
, and
\Delta_{\max}(G)
is the maximum vertex degree in
G
.

Proof.

We obtain an embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
E(G)
into
H
, as follows. First, since
E^{\operatorname{del}}\subseteq E(H^{\prime})
, we embed every edge
e\in E^{\operatorname{del}}
into itself. Each remaining edge
e\in E(G)
must lie in some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. For each such cluster
C
, we use the embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}
of
C
into
C^{\prime}
that is given by
Section

9.1
. Since, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the corresponding graph
C^{\prime}
is contained in
H^{\prime}
, and since the clusters in
{\mathcal{C}}
are edge-disjoint, we obtain an embedding of
G
into
H^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
\tilde{d}
, that cause congestion at most
\eta
.

Finally, by combining
Theorem

1.3
with
Section

9.1
, we obtain the following immediate corollary; the proof is essentially identical to the proof of
Section

9.1
and is omitted here.

Corollary 9.6
.

There is an algorithm, whose input is a graph
G
with
n
vertices and no edges, that undergoes an online sequence of at most
n^{2}
edge deletions and insertions, such that
G
remains a simple graph throughout the update sequence. Each edge inserted into
G
has an integral length
\ell(e)\in\left\{1,\ldots,L\right\}
. Additionally, the algorithm is given parameters
512\leq k\leq(\log n)^{1/49}
,
\frac{1}{k}\leq\delta\leq\frac{1}{400}
and
\Delta\geq n^{20/k}
, such that
k,\Delta
and
\frac{1}{\delta}
are integers. The algorithm maintains a subgraph
H\subseteq G
with
V(H)=V(G)
and
|E(H)|\leq O\left(n^{1+O(1/k)}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log L\right)
, such that
H
is a
(k^{\prime},\eta)
-low-congestion spanner for
G
, for
k^{\prime}=k^{19}\cdot 2^{O(1/\delta^{6})}
and
\eta\leq n^{O(1/k)}\cdot\max\left\{\frac{\Delta_{\max}(G)}{\Delta},1\right\}
, where
\Delta_{\max}(G)
is the maximum degree in
G
. The worst-case update time of the algorithm is
n^{O(\delta)}
per operation, and the number of edge insertions and deletions that graph
H
undergoes after each update to
G
is bounded by
n^{O(1/k)}
.

9.2
Dynamic Fault-Tolerant Spanners and Connectivity Certificates

In this subsection we provide a key observation that routers with a sufficiently large minimum degree are inherently resilient to edge failures. We then show that, by setting the parameter
\Delta
in
Theorem

1.3
appropriately, we can ensure that the resulting graph
H
is resilient to bounded-degree faults. Lastly, we prove that
H
can be used as a connectivity certificate.

9.2.1
Resilience of Routers to Bounded-Degree Faults

Let
G
be a graph, and let
F
be a subset of edges of
G
(edge faults). We denote by
\deg(F)
the maximum, over all vertices of
v
, of the number of edges in
F
that are incident to
v
. We say that a set
F
of edges is an
f
-BD faulty set, if
\deg(F)\leq f
. Recall that a graph
G
is a
(\overline{\Delta},d,\eta)
-router for the set
V(G)
of supported vertices, if every
\Delta
-restricted demand
{\mathcal{D}}
over
V(G)
can be routed with congestion at most
\eta
in
G
, via paths of length at most
d
. In this subsection we show that routers are resilient to bounded-degree faults, and the sense that
G\setminus F
remains a router, albeit with slightly weaker parameters. The main result of this subsection is summarized in the following theorem, whose proof is provided below.

Theorem 9.7
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph, and let
f,k,d>0
,
1\leq\eta<n^{2}
and
\Delta\geq 32f\cdot n^{1/k}\cdot\eta
be parameters. Assume further that
G
is a
(\overline{\Delta},d,\eta)
-router with respect to the set
V(G)
of supported vertices. Then for every
f
-BD faulty set
F\subseteq E(G)
of edges, graph
G\setminus F
is a
(\overline{\Delta},O(k)\cdot d,O(k)\cdot\eta)
-router for the set
V(G)
of supported vertices.

The above theorem should be compared to the resilience of length-constrained expanders against
f
-BD faults, that was established in
[
15
]
. Specifically, they show in Theorem 1.2 of
[
15
]
that, if
G
is an
n
-vertex
(h,s)
-length
\varphi
-expander with minimum degree
\Omega(fn^{\epsilon}/\varphi)
, then, for any
f
-BD faulty set
F\subseteq E(G)
of edges, the graph
G\setminus F
is an
(hs)^{1/\epsilon}
-spanner of
G
; in other words, the distances in
G\setminus F
may grow by at most factor
(hs)^{1/\epsilon}
.
It then follows that the
f
-FD spanners obtained by
[
15
]
via LC-expanders have size
\widetilde{O}(f\cdot n^{1+1/k})
and stretch
t=k^{O(k)}
.
The question of improving the exponential dependence of the stretch on
k
, while preserving the size of the spanner was left open in
[
15
]
(see Theorem 1.14 therein), and this improvement would immediately imply stronger bounds for
f
-FD spanners. We resolve this open problem in
Theorem

9.7
, for the case where the LC-expander has a bounded diameter (that is, it is a router).

Proof of
Theorem

9.7
.

Consider any demand
\hat{\mathcal{D}}=\left(\hat{\Pi},\left\{\hat{D}(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\hat{\Pi}}\right)
. We say that demand
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
is
integral
, if, for every pair
(a,b)\in\hat{\Pi}
, the demand value
\hat{D}(a,b)
is an integer. It will be convenient for us to represent such an integral demand
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
by a multiset
I=\left\{(a_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(a_{\chi},b_{\chi})\right\}
of pairs of vertices, with
\chi=\sum_{(a,b)\in\hat{\Pi}}\hat{D}(a,b)
, where each pair
(a,b)\in\hat{\Pi}
appears in
I
with multiplicity
\hat{D}(a,b)
.
Throughout, we identify each pair (and its two vertices) by its index in the multiset,
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})
. Conversely, any multiset
I
of pairs of vertices in
G
naturally defines the corresponding demand
\hat{\mathcal{D}}(I)
, where the demand
\hat{D}(a,b)
for every pair
(a,b)
is the number of times it appears in
I
. The resulting demand is
\hat{\Delta}
-restricted, for a parameter
\hat{\Delta}
, if every vertex of
G
appears in at most
\hat{\Delta}
pairs in
I
. For convenience, we will not distinguish between the integral demand
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
, and its corresponding multiset
I
of vertex pairs. Consider now an integral demand
I=\left\{(a_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(a_{\chi},b_{\chi})\right\}
. A routing
f
of
I
is
integral
if, for every pair
(a_{i},b_{i})
, there is exactly one flow-path
P
connecting
a_{i}
to
b_{i}
with
f(P)>0
, and moreover,
f(P)=1
. An integral routing of an integral demand
I=\left\{(a_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(a_{\chi},b_{\chi})\right\}
can equivalently be defined as a collection
{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{\chi}\right\}
of paths, where, for all
1\leq i\leq\chi
, path
P_{i}
connects
a_{i}
to
b_{i}
.
We will use the following simple observation.

Observation 9.8
.

Let
\hat{\mathcal{D}}=\left(\hat{\Pi},\left\{\hat{D}(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\hat{\Pi}}\right)
be an integral demand in
G
, and assume that
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
is an
(\alpha\cdot\Delta)
-restricted demand, for some parameter
\alpha\geq 8\log n
. Then there is an integral routing
{\mathcal{P}}
of
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
, where each path in
{\mathcal{P}}
has length at most
d
, and the paths cause congestion at most
8\alpha\eta
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
be a demand that is obtained from
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
, by scaling all demands in
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
down by factor
\alpha
. It is easy to verify that demand
\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
is
\Delta
-restricted. Since
G
is a
(\overline{\Delta},d,\eta)
-router with respect to the set
V(G)
of supported vertices, there is a routing
f^{\prime}
of
\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
in
G
via paths of length at most
d
, that causes congestion at most
\eta
. By scaling the flow value
f^{\prime}(P)
on every flow-path up by factor
\alpha
, we obtain a routing
f
of
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
via paths of length at most
d
, that cause congestion at most
\alpha\eta
. Lastly, in order to convert this routing into an integral one, we use the standard Randomized Rounding procedure of Raghavan and Thompson
[
71
]
.

Consider the multiset
I=\left\{(a_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(a_{\chi},b_{\chi})\right\}
of vertex pairs that corresponds to the demand
\hat{\mathcal{D}}
. We can naturally view the flow
f
as routing the pairs in
I
via paths of length at most
d
, with congestion at most
\alpha\eta
. Consider now
any demand pair
(a_{i},b_{i})\in I
, and let
{\mathcal{P}}_{(a_{i},b_{i})}
be the collection of all
a_{i}
-
b_{i}
paths of length at most
d
in
G
. Since flow
f
sends
1
flow unit from
a_{i}
to
b_{i}
, it defines a probability distribution
D_{i}
over the paths of
{\mathcal{P}}_{(a_{i},b_{i})}
, where every path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}_{(a_{i},b_{i})}
is assigned probability
f(P)
. For all
1\leq i\leq\chi
, we choose a single path
P_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{(a_{i},b_{i})}
from this distribution, where the choices between the different pairs are independent. Let
{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{\chi}\right\}
be the resulting routing. Clearly, the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
have length at most
d
each. Next, we argue that with high probability, the congestion of the routing is at most
8\alpha\eta
. Indeed, the expected congestion for an edge
e\in E(G)
is at most
\alpha\cdot\eta
. Using the Chernoff bound from
Section

2.9
with
t=8\alpha\eta
, we get that the probability that the congestion on
e
is greater than
8\alpha\eta
is bounded by
2^{-8\alpha\eta}<1/n^{3}
, since
\alpha\geq 8\log n
. Using the union bound, with high probability, the congestion on every edge is at most
8\alpha\eta
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}=\left(\Pi,\left\{D(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi}\right)
be a
\Delta
-restricted demand for
G
, and let
F\subseteq E(G)
be an
f
-BD faulty edge set. We will denote by
V(F)
the set of all vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges in
F
. Our goal is to show that
{\mathcal{D}}
can be routed in
G\setminus F
via paths of length at most
O(k)\cdot d
, with congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta
. Our first step is to convert
{\mathcal{D}}
into an integral demand.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}=\left(\Pi,\left\{D^{\prime}(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi}\right)
be the demand obtained from
{\mathcal{D}}
, by setting
D^{\prime}(a,b)=\left\lceil D(a,b)\cdot n\right\rceil
for every pair
(a,b)\in\Pi
of vertices. It is easy to verify, that, since every vertex of
G
may participate in at most
n
pairs in
\Pi
, the demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
is
\Delta^{\prime}
-restricted, for
\Delta^{\prime}=2n\Delta
. Let
\eta^{\prime}=16\eta\cdot n
. Assume that there is a routing
f^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
in
G\setminus F
via paths of length
O(k)\cdot d
, with congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
. Then we can obtain a routing
f
of
{\mathcal{D}}
in
G
via paths of length
O(k)\cdot d
and congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta
, by scaling the flow
f^{\prime}
down by factor
n
, and then reducing it as needed. Therefore, from now on, it is enough to show that there is a routing
f^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
in
G\setminus F
via paths of length
O(k)\cdot d
, with congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
.
Notice that the resulting demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}=\left(\Pi,\left\{D^{\prime}(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi}\right)
is integral. Throughout, we denote by
I=\left\{(a_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(a_{\chi},b_{\chi})\right\}
the corresponding multiset of vertex pairs. Our goal is to send
1
flow unit between every pair
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})
, such that the resulting flow only uses flow-paths of length at most
O(k)\cdot d
, and causes congestion at most
O(k\cdot\eta^{\prime})
.
We will repeatedly use the following immediate corollary of
Section

9.2.1
.

Corollary 9.9
.

If
I^{\prime}
is any integral
\Delta^{\prime}
-restricted demand in
G
, then there is an integral routing
{\mathcal{P}}
of
I^{\prime}
, where each path in
{\mathcal{P}}
has length at most
d
, and the paths cause congestion at most
16\eta n=\eta^{\prime}
.

Our algorithm consists of
z\leq O(k)
iterations. In each iteration
i
, we define a
\Delta^{\prime}
-restricted demand
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
for
V(G)
, and then consider its routing
f_{i}
in
G
. For each flow-path
P
that carries non-zero flow, we discard all edges of
F\cap E(P)
, partitioning
P
into segments, some of which continue to carry
f_{i}(P)
flow units; we denote the resulting flow, that does not use the edges of
F
, by
f^{\prime}_{i}
. The final routing of
{\mathcal{D}}
is obtained by combining the resulting flows
f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{z}
.

Consider now the multiset
I
of vertex pairs, corresponding to the demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
. From
Section

9.2.1
, there is an integral routing
{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{\chi}\right\}
of
I
via paths of length at most
d
, with congestion at most
\eta^{\prime}
, where, for all
1\leq\ell\leq\chi
, path
P_{\ell}
connects
a_{\ell}
to
b_{\ell}
. We say that a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
is
safe
, if
E(P)\cap F=\emptyset
, and we say that it is
unsafe
otherwise. We say that a pair
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\in I
is
safe
if
P_{\ell}
is a safe path, and we say that it is
unsafe
otherwise. From now on, it is sufficient to compute a routing for unsafe pairs in
I
.

Consider an unsafe pair
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\in I
. Let
x_{\ell}

be the vertex of
V(F)\cap V(P_{\ell})
lying closest to
a_{\ell}
on the path, and let
P_{\ell}^{1}
be the subpath of
P_{\ell}
from
a_{\ell}
to
x_{\ell}
. Similarly, let
y_{\ell}

be the vertex of
V(F)\cap V(P_{\ell})
lying closest to
b_{\ell}
on the path, and let
P_{\ell}^{2}
be the subpath of
P_{\ell}
from
y_{\ell}
to
b_{\ell}
. Notice that both
P_{\ell}^{1}
and
P_{\ell}^{2}
are safe paths.
Consider the following multiset of vertex pairs:

I^{\prime}=\left\{(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\mid P_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{P}},P_{\ell}\cap F\neq\emptyset\right\}

In the remainder of the proof, we will compute an integral routing of the pairs in
I^{\prime}
in
G\setminus F
via paths of length at most
O(k)\cdot d
, that cause congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
. From the following observation, such a routing will complete the proof of
Theorem

9.7
.

Observation 9.10
.

Suppose there is an integral routing
{\mathcal{Q}}
of the demand
I^{\prime}
in
G\setminus F
via paths of length at most
O(k)\cdot d
that cause congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
. Then there is a routing
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
of the demand
I
in
G\setminus F
via paths of length at most
O(k)\cdot d
, that cause congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For every unsafe pair
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\in I
, let
Q_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
be the path connecting
x_{\ell}
to
y_{\ell}
. Let
P^{\prime}_{\ell}
be the path obtained by concatenating the paths
P^{1}_{\ell},Q_{\ell}
, and
P^{2}_{\ell}
. Then path
P^{\prime}_{\ell}
is contained in
G\setminus F
, it connects
a_{\ell}
to
b_{\ell}
, and its length is at most
O(k)\cdot d
.
For every safe pair
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\in I
, we simply set
P^{\prime}_{\ell}=P_{\ell}
. It is also immediate to verify that the resulting set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=\left\{P^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,P^{\prime}_{\chi}\right\}
of paths causes congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
, and it routes the pairs in
I
in graph
G\setminus F
.

In the remainder of the proof, we show that there exists an integral routing of the demands in
I^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
O(k)\cdot d
, that cause congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
. We construct such a routing over the course of
z=10k
iterations.
For all
1\leq i\leq z
, at the beginning of iteration
i
, we are given a partition of the multiset
I^{\prime}
into two subsets: set
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
of pairs that we refer to as
i
-safe
, and set
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
of pairs that we refer to as
i
-unsafe
. The set
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
of
i
-safe pairs is guaranteed to satisfy the following property:

Q1.

there is an integral routing
{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
of the pairs in
\mathcal{S}_{i}
in graph
G\setminus F
via paths of length at most
3i\cdot d
, that cause congestion at most
2i\cdot\eta^{\prime}
.

For all
1\leq i\leq z
, we denote by
V^{\prime}_{i}=\{x_{\ell},y_{\ell}\}_{(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\in\mathcal{U}_{i}}
the multiset of all vertices that appear in the
i
-unsafe pairs. For every vertex
v\in V^{\prime}_{i}
, we will construct a tree-like structure (that, for brevity, we refer to as a tree), denoted by
T_{i}(v)
, whose root vertex is
v
, and all other vertices lie in
V(F)
. Graph
T_{i}(v)
is not a tree strictly speaking, in the sense that a vertex of
V(F)
may appear multiple times in the tree. But if each such appearence is replaced with a new copy of the vertex, then we naturally obtain a tree graph, that is denoted by
T_{i}(v)
. We note that, if a vertex
v
appears in several pairs in
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
, then we construct a separate tree for each such appearance of
v
, so
v
will serve as a root for several such trees.
Throughout, we use a parameter
\lambda=\left\lfloor\frac{\Delta^{\prime}}{f\cdot\eta^{\prime}}\right\rfloor
.
We ensure that, for all
1\leq i\leq z
, at the beginning of iteration
i
, the following properties hold for every vertex
v\in V^{\prime}_{i}
:

Q2.

T_{i}(v)
is a
\lambda
-regular tree of depth
i-1
; and

Q3.

the multiset
L_{i}(v)
of the leaf vertices of
T_{i}(v)
has cardinality
\lambda^{i-1}
.

Additionally, the collection
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}=\{T_{i}(w)\}_{w\in V^{\prime}_{i}}
of trees satisfies the following properties:

Q4.

every vertex of
V(F)
appears with multiplicity at most
f\eta^{\prime}
in the union of multisets
\{L_{i}(v)\}_{v\in V^{\prime}_{i}}
; and

Q5.

For every tree
T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
, for every edge
e=(u,w)\in E(T)
, there is a path
R(e)
connecting
u
to
w
in
G\setminus F
of length at most
d
, such that the paths in set
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}=\left\{R(e)\mid e\in E(T),T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\right\}
cause congestion at most
i\eta^{\prime}
.

We now show that, if the above invariants are always obeyed, then, for some
j\leq 10k
,
{\mathcal{U}}_{j}=\emptyset
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{j}=I^{\prime}
holds.

Claim 9.11
.

Assume that Invariants
Q1
–
Q5
are always obeyed. Then, for some
1\leq j\leq 10k
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{S}_{j}=I^{\prime}
must hold.

Proof.

Assume for contradiction that the claim is false, and so
\mathcal{U}_{10k}\neq\emptyset
must hold. Consider any pair
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\in\mathcal{U}_{10k}
.
Recall that
\lambda=\left\lfloor\frac{\Delta^{\prime}}{f\cdot\eta^{\prime}}\right\rfloor
, while

\Delta^{\prime}=2n\Delta\geq 64fn^{1+1/k}\cdot\eta\geq 2fn^{1/k}\eta^{\prime}
, since

\eta^{\prime}=16\eta\cdot n
. Therefore,
\lambda=\left\lfloor\frac{\Delta^{\prime}}{f\cdot\eta^{\prime}}\right\rfloor\geq 2n^{1/k}-1
. From Invariant
Q3
, the multiset
L_{10k}(x_{\ell})
of the leaf vertices of
T_{i}(x_{\ell})
has cardinality at least
\lambda^{10k-1}>2n^{5}
. However, from Invariant
Q4
, every vertex of
V(F)
appears with multiplicity at most
f\eta^{\prime}\leq 2n^{4}
in
L_{10k}(x_{\ell})
. Therefore, the number of distinct vertices in

L_{10k}(x_{\ell})
is strictly greater than
n
, leading to a contradiction. Since multisets
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathcal{S}_{j}
partition the multiset
I^{\prime}
, the claim follows.

At the beginning of the algorithm, we set
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}=\emptyset
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}=\emptyset
, and
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}=I^{\prime}
. As before, we let
V_{1}^{\prime}
be the multiset of all vertices that participate in the pairs in
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
. For every vertex
v\in V_{1}^{\prime}
, we let
T_{1}(v)
be a tree of depth
0
, that only consists of the root vertex
v
. It is immediate to verify that
Invariants
Q1
–
Q3
and
Q5
hold. In order to see that Invariant
Q4
holds as well, recall that the collection
{\mathcal{P}}
of paths that defined the original routing of the demands in
I
had congestion at most
\eta^{\prime}
. Consider any vertex
u\in V(F)
. The number of times that
u
appears in the trees of
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}=\left\{T_{1}(v)\mid v\in V_{1}^{\prime}\right\}
is bounded by the number of paths
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, such that
P
contains an edge of the faulty set
F
that is incident to
u
. Since at most
f
edges incident to
u
may lie in
F
, and since each such edge participates in at most
\eta^{\prime}
paths, vertex
u
may serve as the root of at most
f\cdot\eta^{\prime}
trees in
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
.

The algorithm is executed as long as
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\neq\emptyset
holds. We now describe the
i
th iteration. We assume that we are given a partition
({\mathcal{S}}_{i},{\mathcal{U}}_{i})
of the set
I^{\prime}
of pairs, the corresponding multiset
V^{\prime}_{i}
of vertices, and the collection
{\mathcal{T}}_{i}=\left\{T_{i}(v)\mid v\in V^{\prime}_{i}\right\}
of trees, for which invariants
Q1
–
Q5
hold. We now provide the description of the
i
th iteration.

Desciption of the
i
th Iteration.

For every pair
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
, we construct a new demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{\ell}
, as follows. Consider the multisets
L_{i}(x_{\ell})
and
L_{i}(y_{\ell})
of vertices (the leaf vertices of the trees
T_{i}(x_{\ell})
and
T_{i}(y_{\ell})
, respectively). From
Invariant
Q3
,
|L_{i}(x_{\ell})|=|L_{i}(y_{\ell})|=\lambda^{i-1}
. Let
\Pi^{i}_{\ell}
be an arbitrary complete matching between the vertices of
L_{i}(x_{\ell})
and the vertices of
L_{i}(y_{\ell})
. For every pair
(u,v)\in\Pi^{i}_{\ell}
, we set the demand
D^{i}_{\ell}(u,v)=\lambda
. Since
\lambda
is an integer, the resulting demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{\ell}=\left(\Pi^{i}_{\ell},\left\{D^{i}_{\ell}(u,v)\right\}_{(u,v)\in\Pi^{i}_{\ell}}\right)
is integral. We then let
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}=\left(\Pi^{i},\left\{D^{i}(u,v)\right\}_{(u,v)\in\Pi^{i}}\right)
be the demand obtained by taking the union of the demands
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{\ell}
for all
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
. Next, we prove that the demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}
is
\Delta^{\prime}
-restricted.

Observation 9.12
.

Demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}
is
\Delta^{\prime}
-restricted.

Proof.

Notice that demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}
is only defined over the vertices of
V(F)
. Consider any such vertex
v
. From Invariant
Q4
, the number of copies of
v
that appear in the sets
L_{i}(w)
for
w\in V^{\prime}_{i}
is at most
f\eta^{\prime}
. Each such copy appears in a single demand pair with demand
\lambda
. Therefore, the total demand on vertex
v
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}
is bounded by
\lambda\cdot f\cdot\eta^{\prime}\leq\Delta^{\prime}
, since
\lambda=\left\lfloor\frac{\Delta^{\prime}}{f\cdot\eta^{\prime}}\right\rfloor
. We conclude that demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}
is
\Delta^{\prime}
-restricted.

Since demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}
is
\Delta^{\prime}
-restricted, from
Section

9.2.1
, there is an integral routing
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
of
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}
in
G
via paths of length at most
d
, that cause congestion at most
\eta^{\prime}
.

Consider now a pair
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
, and a corresponding demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{\ell}
. Let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{\ell}\subseteq{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
be the collection of
\lambda^{i}
paths routing the demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{\ell}
. We say that the pair
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})
is
good
, if at least one path
P\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{\ell}
is disjoint from
E(F)
, and we say that it is bad otherwise.

Consider a single good pair
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
, and let
P^{\prime}_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{\ell}
be any path that is disjoint from
E(F)
. Denote the endpoints of
P^{\prime}_{\ell}
by
w
and
w^{\prime}
, where
w\in L_{i}(x_{\ell})
, and
w^{\prime}\in L_{i}(y_{\ell})
, respectively. Let
\hat{P}_{1}
be the path in the tree
T_{i}(x_{\ell})
, connecting
x_{\ell}
to
w
, so the number of edges on
\hat{P}_{1}
is at most
i
. We obtain a path
P^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}
in graph
G\setminus F
connecting
x_{\ell}
to
w
, by replacing every edge
e\in E(\hat{P}_{1})
with its embedding path
R(e)\in{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
given in Invariant
Q5
. Since the length of every path
R(e)
is at most
d
, we get that the length of
P^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}
is a tmost
id
. We use a similar procedure to compute a path
P^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\ell}
in
G\setminus F
connecting
w^{\prime}
to
y_{\ell}
. By concatenating the paths
P^{\prime\prime}_{\ell},P^{\prime}_{\ell}
and
P^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\ell}
, we obtain a path
P_{\ell}
in
G\setminus F
connecting
x_{\ell}
to
y_{\ell}
, whose length is at most
3id
. Consider now the collection
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{i}
of paths, that contains a path
P_{\ell}
for every good pair
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
. From our discussion, all such paths are contained in
G\setminus F
, and each such path has length at most
3id
. Since, from Invariant
Q5
, the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
cause congestion at most
\eta^{\prime}\cdot i
, and since the routing

{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
has congestion at most
\eta^{\prime}
, it is immediate to verify that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{i}
cause congestion at most
(i+1)\eta^{\prime}
. We let set
{\mathcal{S}}_{i+1}
contain all pairs of
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
, and all good pairs that lie in
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
. We also let
{\mathcal{P}}_{i+1}={\mathcal{P}}_{i}\cup{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}_{i}
.
From Invariant
Q1
and our discussion above, the set
{\mathcal{P}}_{i+1}
of paths causes congstion at most
2(i+1)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
, and so Invariant
Q1
continues to hold.

We let
{\mathcal{U}}_{i+1}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
be the set of all bad pairs
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})
, and we let
V_{i+1}^{\prime}
be the multisetset of all vertices that participate in the pairs in
U_{i+1}
. For every vertex
v\in V_{i+1}^{\prime}
, we extend the tree
T_{i}(v)
, in order to obtain the tree
T_{i+1}(v)
with the required properties.

Consider a vertex
v\in V_{i+1}^{\prime}
, and assume that it belongs to a pair
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\in U_{i+1}
. Let
L=L_{i}(v)
be the set of all leaf vertices of the tree
T_{i}(v)
, and let
u\in L
be any such vertex. Recall that there is some pair
(u,w)\in\Pi^{i}_{\ell}
in which
u
participates, and moreover, this pair is assigned demand
\lambda
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{i}_{\ell}
. Therefore, there is a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}(u)\subseteq{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}_{\ell}
of
\lambda
paths that originate at
u
. Consider any such path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}(u)
, and recall that it must contain an edge of
F
. Let
u^{\prime}\in V(F)\cap V(Q)
be the vertex lying closes to
u
on path
Q
, and let
R(u,u^{\prime})
be the subpath of
Q
from
u
to
u^{\prime}
. Then
R(u,u^{\prime})
is disjoint from
F
, and
u^{\prime}\in V(F)
. We add vertex
u^{\prime}
as a child vertex of
u
in the tree
T_{i}(v)
, and we let the embedding path of the new edge
(u,u^{\prime})
be the path
R(u,u^{\prime})
defined above; note that the length of the path is at most
d
. Once we process all leaf vertices of
T_{i}(v)
, we obtain a new tree
T_{i+1}(v)
, that remains a
\lambda
-regular tree, whose depth is
i+1
. From our construction, its set of leaf vertices
L_{i+1}(v)
has cardinality
\lambda\cdot|L_{i}(v)|=\lambda^{i}
. For every edge
e
of the tree, if it lied in
T_{i}(v)
, then its embedding path
R(e)\in{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
is as defined in Invariant
Q5
, and otherwise, its embedding path is a subpath of one of the routing paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
as defined above. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}_{i}
caused congestion at most
i\eta^{\prime}
, while the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
cause congestion at most
\eta^{\prime}
, it is easy to verify that the resulting set
{\mathcal{R}}_{i+1}=\left\{R(e)\mid e\in E(T_{i+1}(v)),v\in V_{i+1}^{\prime}\right\}
cause congestion at most
(i+1)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
.

So far we have shown that
Invariants
Q1
–
Q3
and
Q5
hold. It now only remains to establish Invariant
Q4
.
Indeed, recall that the collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
of paths causes congestion at most
\eta^{\prime}
. Consider any vertex
u\in V(F)
. The number of times that
u
appears in the sets
L_{i+1}(v)
of vertices for
v\in V^{\prime}_{i+1}
is bounded by the number of paths
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, such that
Q
contains an edge of the faulty set
F
that is incident to
u
. Since at most
f
edges incident to
u
may lie in
F
, and since each such edge participates in at most
\eta^{\prime}
paths, vertex
u
may appear at
f\cdot\eta^{\prime}
times in the sets
\left\{L_{i+1}(v)\right\}_{v\in V^{\prime}_{i+1}}
.

Note that, when the algorithm terminates, which must happen after
i\leq 10k
iterations, all pairs of
I^{\prime}
lie in the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
of
i
-safe pairs. From Invariant
Q1
, we conclude that there is an integral routing of the pairs in
I^{\prime}
in graph
G\setminus F
via paths of length at most
O(k)\cdot d
, that cause congestion at most
O(k)\cdot\eta^{\prime}
, as required.

Figure 1:
An illustration for the proof of Lemma
9.7
. Shown is an
i
-unsafe pair
(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})
with their corresponding trees
T_{i}(x_{\ell})
and
T_{i}(y_{\ell})
. The routing instance for iteration
i
is defined by sending
\lambda
flow units between matched leaf vertices in
L_{i}(x_{\ell})
and
L_{i}(y_{\ell})
. The solid edges correspond to
\lambda
units of demand between the matched pair.

9.2.2
From Router Decomposition to Fault Tolerant Spanners

Fault-Tolerant Spanners.

Recall that for a graph
G
, a stretch parameter
k\geq 1
, and an integer fault parameter
f\geq 1
, an
f
-FT
k
-spanner of
G
is a subgraph
H\subseteq G
satisfying that, for every subset
F\subseteq E(G)
of at most
f
edges,
H\setminus F
is a
k
-spanner for
G\setminus F
.

Very recently, Bodwin, Haeupler and Parter
[
16
]
introduced a considerably stronger notion of fault-tolerance, where only the degree of the faulty edge set, rather than its cardinality, is restricted.
For a subset
F\subseteq E(G)
of faulty edges, its
faulty-degree

\deg(F)
is the maximum, over all vertices
v
, of the number of edges of
F
incident to
v
. For example, if
F
is a matching, then
\deg(F)=1
, while
|F|
may be as large as
n/2
. This naturally leads to the definition of faulty-degree spanners.

Definition 9.13
(Faulty-Degree Spanners).

[
16
]

Let
G
be a graph and let
H\subseteq G
be a subgraph of
G
with
V(H)=V(G)
. For parameters
f
and
k>0
, we say that
H
is an
f
-faulty-degree (FD)
k
-spanner for
G
if, for every subset
F\subseteq E(G)
of edges with
\deg(F)\leq f
, for every pair
u,v
of vertices of
G
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H\setminus F}(u,v)\leq k\cdot\mbox{\sf dist}_{G\setminus F}(u,v)
holds.

In the following lemma, we show that, if
H^{\prime}
is a graph obtained from a router decomposition of the graph
G
as before, then it is an
f
-FD
k^{\prime}
-spanner, for appropriately chosen parameters
f
and
k^{\prime}
.

Observation 9.14
(From Router Decomposition to
f
-FD Spanners).

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph, and let
k^{\prime}>0
and
f\in\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
be parameters. Let
\mathcal{R}=\left(\mathcal{C},\{C^{\prime}\}_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\right)
be a router decomposition of
G
with parameters
\tilde{d}
,
1\leq\tilde{\eta}\leq n^{2}
,
\Delta^{*}\geq 32\cdot f\cdot\tilde{\eta}\cdot n^{1/k^{\prime}}
, and
\rho
.
Consider the corresponding graph
H^{\prime}
whose vertex set is
V(G)
, and edge set is
E^{\operatorname{del}}\cup\left(\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}E(C^{\prime})\right)
, where
E^{\operatorname{del}}=E(G)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}E(C)\right)
. Then
H^{\prime}
is an
f
-FD
O(\tilde{d}\cdot k^{\prime})
-spanner.

Proof.

Consider an
f
-BD faulty set
F\subseteq E(G)
of edges, and an edge
e=(u,v)\in E(G)\setminus F
. If
e\in E^{\operatorname{del}}
, then
e
is included in
H^{\prime}
, and so
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H^{\prime}\setminus F}(u,v)=1
. Otherwise, there must be some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, with
u,v\in C
. Consider the corresponding subgraph
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
, and recall that
V(C)=V(C^{\prime})
, and
C^{\prime}
is a
(\overline{\Delta}^{*},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
-router for the set
V(C)
of supported vertices. Then from
Theorem

9.7
, graph
C^{\prime}\setminus F
remains a
(\overline{\Delta}^{*},O(k\cdot\tilde{d}),O(k\cdot\tilde{\eta}))
-router. In particular, the diameter of
C^{\prime}\setminus F
is bounded by
O(k^{\prime}\tilde{d})
which implies that

\mbox{\sf dist}_{C^{\prime}\setminus F}(u,v)=O(k^{\prime}\tilde{d})
. Since
E(C^{\prime})\subseteq H^{\prime}
, the observation follows.

Fault-Tolerant Low-Congestion Spanners.

Finally, we show the strongest property of subgraph
H
maintained by the algorithm from Theorem
1.3
. We will use the fact that routers retain their routing properties (with a small loss), to show that
H
is a fault-tolerant low-congestion spanner even against bounded degree faults. We start by formally defining the latter notion.

Definition 9.15
(Fault-Tolerant Low-Congestion Spanners).

Let
G
be a graph, let
H\subseteq G
be a subgraph of
G
, and let
f,d
and
\eta
be parameters. We say that
H
is an
f
-FT
(d,\eta)
-low congestion spanner if, for every subset
F\subseteq E(G)
of edges with
|F|\leq f
, graph
G\setminus F
embeds into
H\setminus F
via paths of length at most
d
, with congestion at most
\eta
. Similarly,
H
is an
f
-FD
(d,\eta)
-low congestion spanner if the above holds for every subset
F\subseteq E(G)
of edges with
\deg(F)\leq f
.

In the following observation we show that the graph
H^{\prime}
obtained from a router decomposition of
G
with an appropriately chosen parameter
f
must be an
f
-FD low-congestion spanner.

Observation 9.16
(From Router Decomposition to
f
-FD Low-Congestion Spanners).

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph, and let
k^{\prime}>0
and
f\in\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
be parameters. Let
\mathcal{R}=\left(\mathcal{C},\{C^{\prime}\}_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\right)
be a router decomposition of
G
with parameters
\tilde{d}
,
1\leq\tilde{\eta}\leq n^{2}
,
\Delta^{*}\geq 32\cdot f\cdot\tilde{\eta}\cdot n^{1/k^{\prime}}
, and
\rho
.
Consider the corresponding graph
H^{\prime}
whose vertex set is
V(G)
, and edge set is
E^{\operatorname{del}}\cup\left(\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}E(C^{\prime})\right)
, where
E^{\operatorname{del}}=E(G)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}E(C)\right)
. Then
H^{\prime}
is an
f
-FD
(d,\eta)
-low congestion spanner for
d=O(k^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{d})
and
\eta=\max\left\{O\left(\frac{k^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{\eta}\cdot\Delta_{\max}(G)}{\Delta^{*}}\right),16\log n\right\}
, where
\Delta_{\max}(G)
is maximum vertex degree in
G
.

Proof.

Let
F\subseteq E(G)
be a set of edges with
\deg(F)\leq f
. Our goal is to show that
H^{\prime}\setminus F
is a
(d,\eta)
-low congestion spanner for
G\setminus F
. In other words, we need to show that
G\setminus F
can be embedded into
H^{\prime}\setminus F
via paths of length at most
d
and congestion at most
\eta
. Since
E^{\operatorname{del}}\subseteq E(H^{\prime})
, and the clusters of
\mathcal{C}
are edge-disjoint, it is sufficient to show that, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, there is an embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}
of
C\setminus F
into
C^{\prime}\setminus F
via paths of length at most
d
, that cause congestion at most
\eta
.

Since, for every cluster
C\in\mathcal{C}
, the corresponding graph
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
is a
(\overline{\Delta}^{*},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{d})
router for the set
V(C)=V(C^{\prime})
of supported vertices, and
\Delta^{*}\geq 32fn^{1/k^{\prime}}\cdot\tilde{\eta}
holds, from
Theorem

9.7
we get that that
C^{\prime}\setminus F
is a
(\Delta^{*},O(k^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{d}),O(k^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{\eta}))
-router. In particular,
\left({\mathcal{C}},\left\{C^{\prime}\setminus F\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\right)
remains a router decomposition of
G
with parameters
\Delta^{*},O(k^{\prime})\cdot\tilde{d}
,
O(k^{\prime})\cdot\tilde{\eta}
, and
\rho
. From Lemma
9.1
, there is an embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}
of
C
into
C^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
O(k^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{d})
, that cause congetion at most

\max\left\{O\left(\frac{k^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{\eta}\cdot\Delta_{\max}(G)}{\Delta^{*}}\right),16\log n\right\}
.

Finally, we obtain the following analogue of Corollaries
9.1
and

9.1
for
f
-FD low-congestion spanners.

Corollary 9.17
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, whose input is a graph
G
with
n
vertices and no edges, that undergoes an online sequence of at most
n^{2}
edge deletions and insertions, such that
G
remains a simple graph throughout the update sequence. Each edge inserted into
G
has an integral length
\ell(e)\in\left\{1,\ldots,L\right\}
. Additionally, the algorithm is given parameters
512\leq k\leq(\log n)^{1/49}
,
\frac{1}{k}\leq\delta\leq\frac{1}{400}
,
f\in\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
, and
\Delta\geq f\cdot n^{c/k}
, for a large enough constant
c
, such that
k,\Delta
and
\frac{1}{\delta}
are integers. The algorithm maintains a subgraph
H\subseteq G
with
V(H)=V(G)
and
|E(H)|\leq O\left(n^{1+O(1/k)}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log L\right)
, such that
H
is an a
f
-FD
(d,\eta)
-low congestion spanner for
G
, for
d\leq\operatorname{poly}(k)\cdot 2^{O(1/\delta^{6})}
and
\eta\leq n^{O(1/k)}\cdot\max\left\{\frac{\Delta_{\max}(G)}{\Delta},1\right\}
, where
\Delta_{\max}(G)
is the maximum degree in
G
. The worst-case update time of the algorithm is
n^{O(\delta)}
per operation, and the number of edge insertions and deletions that graph
H
undergoes after each update to
G
is bounded by
n^{O(1/k)}
.

In order to prove the corollary, we use the algorithm from
Section

9.1
. By using
Section

9.2.2
with parameter
k^{\prime}
replaced with
k
, it is easy to verify that the graph
H
that the algorithm maintains has all required properties.

Dynamic Connectivity Certificates.

The notion of connectivity certificates was introduced by
[
63
]
.
For a graph
G=(V,E)
and integer
f\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, an
f
-connectivity certificate is a subgraph
H\subseteq G
with the following property: for every pair
u,v\in V
of vertices, for every subset
F\subseteq E
of at most
f
edges,
u
and
v
are connected in
H\setminus F
if and only if they are connected in
G\setminus F
. The recent work of
[
16
]
introduced a stronger notion of
f
-FD connectivity certificate: a subgraph
H\subseteq G
that satisfies the above requirement for any subset
F\subseteq E
of edges with
\deg(F)\leq f
. It is well known that any
n
-vertex graph admits an
f
-connectivity certificate containing
O(fn)
edges, and such certificates can be computed in linear time in the static setting.
Using expander decompositions and expander routing,
[
16
]
presented an algorithm that constructs an
f
-FD connectivity certificates with
\widetilde{O}(fn)
edges, which is nearly tight, in polynomial time.

Clearly, if a graph
H
is an
f
-FD
(d,\eta)
-low congestion spanner for
G
, for any parameters
d
and
\eta
, then
H
is an
f
-FD connectivity certificate and an
f
-connectivity certificate for
G
as well. Therefore, by using the algorithm from
Section

9.2.2
with a parameter
\Delta=f\cdot n^{O(1/k)}
and
\delta=\frac{1}{k}
, we get that the graph
H
that the algorithm maintains has
|E(H)|\leq O\left(f\cdot n^{1+O(1/k)}\right)
, and it is an
f
-FD connectivity certificate for
G
. The algorithm has
n^{O(1/k)}
worst-case update time and
n^{O(1/k)}
recourse. Note that both of the latter quantities are independent of
f
. To the best of our knowledge, all previous dynamic algorithms for (the classical notion of)
f
-connectivity certificates had a worst-case update time with a polynomial dependency on
f
. Therefore, for high values of the parameter
f
, we obtain a faster algorithm.

Appendix A
Proof of
Section

2.4.1

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph, and assume that it is
(d,\eta)
-well-connected with respect to a set
S\subseteq V(G)
of supported vertices. Let
1\leq k\leq\log n
be a parameter. Our goal is to prove that
G
is a
(1,d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
-router with respect to
S
.

Consider any demand
{\mathcal{D}}=\left(\Pi,\left\{D(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi}\right)
. We say that
{\mathcal{D}}
is a
matching
demand, if
\Pi
is a matching, and, for all
(a,b)\in\Pi
,
D(a,b)=1
. Notice that any matching demand can be specified by just the set
\Pi
of pairs of its vertices, so for a matching demand
{\mathcal{D}}
, abusing the notation, we may denote
{\mathcal{D}}=\Pi
.
We show that it is enough to prove that any matching demand can be routed via paths of length at most
d^{\prime}
with congestion at most
\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{16\log n}
, in the following simple claim.

Claim A.1
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph with a set
S\subseteq V(G)
of supported vertices, and assume that, for every matching demand
{\mathcal{D}}
defined over
S
, there is a routing of
{\mathcal{D}}
in
G
via paths of length at most
\hat{d}
and congestion at most
\hat{\eta}
. Then
G
is a
(\overline{1},\hat{d},16\hat{\eta}\cdot\log n)
-router with respect to
S
.

Proof.

Consider any
1
-restricted demand
{\mathcal{D}}=\left(\Pi,\left\{D(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi}\right)
over the vertices of
S
. Let
q=\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil+1
.
For all
1\leq i<q
, we define a demand
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}=\left(\Pi_{i},\left\{D^{\prime}(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi_{i}}\right)
as follows: set
\Pi_{i}
contains all pairs
(a,b)
with
\frac{1}{2^{i}}\leq D(a,b)<\frac{1}{2^{i-1}}
, and
D^{\prime}(a,b)=\frac{1}{2^{i}}
. Additionally, we define a demand
{\mathcal{D}}_{q}=\left(\Pi_{q},\left\{D^{\prime}(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi_{q}}\right)
, where
\Pi_{q}
contains all pairs
(a,b)
with
D(a,b)<\frac{1}{n}
, and we set
D^{\prime}(a,b)=\frac{1}{n}
. It is easy to verify that, for all
1\leq i\leq q
, demand
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is
1
-restricted. Moreover, it is now enough to prove that each such demand
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
can be routed in
G
via paths of length at most
\hat{d}
and congestion at most
4\hat{\eta}
.

From now on we focus on any such demand
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, that we denote by
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}=\left(\Pi^{\prime},\left\{D^{\prime}(a,b)\right\}_{(a,b)\in\Pi^{\prime}}\right)
for convenience. Recall that there is an integer
z
, so that
D^{\prime}(a,b)=\frac{1}{z}
for all
(a,b)\in\Pi^{\prime}
, and that demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
is
1
-restricted. Therefore, every vertex of
S
participates in at most
z
pairs in
\Pi^{\prime}
. We can then compute a collection
\Pi^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Pi^{\prime}_{2z+1}
of pairs of vertices that partition
\Pi^{\prime}
, such that, for all
1\leq j\leq 2z+1
, set
\Pi^{\prime}_{j}
is a matching, using a standard greedy algorithm.

From our assumption, for all
1\leq j\leq 2z+1
, there is a flow
f_{j}
that routes the matching demand between the pairs of vertices in
\Pi^{\prime}_{j}
, where, for each pair
(a,b)\in\Pi^{\prime}_{j}
of vertices, one flow unit is routed from
a
to
b
in
f_{j}
. Moreover, all flow-paths in
f_{j}
have length at most
\hat{d}
, and the flow causes congestion at most
\hat{\eta}
. By scaling each such flow
f_{j}
down by factor
z
(so it causes congestion at most
\frac{\hat{\eta}}{z}
), and taking the union of all such flows, for all
1\leq j\leq 2z+1
, we obtain a flow
f
that routes the demand
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
\hat{d}
, with congestion at most
4\hat{\eta}
.

From now on, it is enough to prove that, for every matching demand
{\mathcal{D}}
defined over
S
, there is a routing of
{\mathcal{D}}
in
G
via paths of length at most
d^{\prime}
, with congestion at most
\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{16\log n}
.

Consider any matching demand
{\mathcal{D}}=\Pi
. We say that
{\mathcal{D}}
is
half-routable
, if there is a subset
\Pi^{\prime}\subseteq\Pi
with
|\Pi^{\prime}|\geq\frac{|\Pi|}{2}
, such that there is a routing of
\Pi^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
d^{\prime}
, and congestion at most
\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{32\log^{2}n}
. It is easy to see that, if every matching demand
{\mathcal{D}}
defined over the set
S
of vertices is half-routable in
G
, then
G
is a
(\overline{1},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
-router with respect to
S
, as we show in the next observation.

Observation A.2
.

Suppose that every matching demand
{\mathcal{D}}
defined over the vertices of
S
is half-routable in
G
. Then
G
is a
(\overline{1},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
-router with respect to
S
.

Proof.

We assume that every matching demand defined over the vertices of
S
is half-routable in
G
. From
Appendix

A
, it is enough to prove that, for every matching demand
{\mathcal{D}}
defined over
S
, there is a routing of
{\mathcal{D}}
in
G
via paths of length at most
d^{\prime}
, that cause congestion at most
\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{16\log n}
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}=\Pi
be any matching demand. We perform at most
\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
iterations, where in the
i
th iteration we route a subset
\Pi_{i}\subseteq\Pi
of the demand pairs, which are then deleted from
\Pi
.

Specifically, the input to the
i
th iteration is the current set
\Pi
of pairs. Since any matching demand is half-routable, there is a subset
\Pi_{i}\subseteq\Pi
, containing at least
\frac{|\Pi|}{2}
pairs, and a routing
f_{i}
of
\Pi_{i}
in
G
via paths of length at most
d^{\prime}
, that causes congestion at most
\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{32\log^{2}n}
. We then delete the pairs of
\Pi_{i}
from
\Pi
, and continue to the next iteration. It is immediate to verify that, after at most
\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
such iterations, set
\Pi
becomes empty, and the union of the resulting flows
f_{i}
provides a routing of
\Pi
, via paths of length at most
d^{\prime}
, and congestion at most
\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil\cdot\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{32\log^{2}n}\leq\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{16\log n}
.

In order to complete the proof of
Section

2.4.1
, it is now enough to prove that every matching demand
{\mathcal{D}}
defined over the set
S
of supported vertices is half-routable. Assume otherwise, and let
{\mathcal{D}}=\Pi
be a matching demand that is not half-routable. Consider the following iterative process for constructing a collection
{\mathcal{P}}
of paths routing some pairs in
\Pi
. We start from
{\mathcal{P}}=\emptyset
, and then iterate, as long as there is a pair
(a,b)\in\Pi
, for which there is a path of length at most
d^{\prime}
connecting
a
to
b
in
G
. In each iteration, we select any such pair
(a,b)\in\Pi
, and any path
P
of length at most
d^{\prime}
connecting
a
to
b
in
G
, add
P
to
{\mathcal{P}}
, delete the pair
(a,b)
from
\Pi
, and delete from
G
every edge that has been used in at least
\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{32\log^{2}n}
of the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
.

Let
\Pi^{\prime}
be the collection of paths that remain in
\Pi
at the end of this process. Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of edges deleted from
G
, and let
G^{\prime}=G\setminus E^{\prime}
. Recall that an edge
e
lies in
E^{\prime}
if and only if it has been used by at least
\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{32\log^{2}n}
of the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
. Therefore,
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|\Pi|\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot 32\log^{2}n}{\eta^{\prime}}
. Note also that
{\mathcal{P}}
is a routing of the pairs in
\Pi\setminus\Pi^{\prime}
via paths of length at most
d^{\prime}
, that cause congestion at most
\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{32\log^{2}n}
. Since demand
{\mathcal{D}}
is not half-routable, we get that
|\Pi^{\prime}|\geq\frac{|\Pi|}{2}
, and, in particular,
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{64|\Pi^{\prime}|\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\eta^{\prime}}
. Lastly, for every pair
(a,b)\in\Pi^{\prime}
,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G^{\prime}}(a,b)\geq d^{\prime}
.

We use the following claim that easily follows from Lemma 3.10 in
[
26
]
, by setting the parameter
\alpha
to
1/2
and parameter
\Delta
to
64k
:

Lemma A.3
(Lemma 3.10 from
[
26
]
).

There is an efficient algorithm, whose input is a graph
G
, a set
T\subseteq V(G)
of
z
vertices called terminals, a distance parameter
\tilde{d}
, and another parameter
k>0
. The algorithm either computes a terminal
t\in T
with
|B_{G}(t,64k\tilde{d})\cap T|>\frac{z}{2}
, or it computes two subsets
T_{1},T_{2}
of terminals, with
|T_{1}|=|T_{2}|
, such that
|T_{1}|\geq\frac{z^{1-1/k}}{3}
, and for every pair
t\in T_{1}
,
t^{\prime}\in T_{2}
of terminals,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G}(t,t^{\prime})\geq\tilde{d}
.

We apply the lemma to graph
G^{\prime}
, with the set
T
of terminals containing all vertices that participate in the pairs of
\Pi^{\prime}
, so that
z=|T|=2|\Pi^{\prime}|
, and parameter
\tilde{d}
replaced by
2d
, and parameter
k
that remains unchanged. Recall that
d^{\prime}=512kd
. Note that it is impossible that the algorithm from
Appendix

A
returns a terminal
t\in T
with
|B_{G^{\prime}}(t,64k\tilde{d})|>\frac{z}{2}
, since in this case it would mean that there is a pair
(a,b)\in\Pi^{\prime}
of vertices, with both
a,b\in B_{G^{\prime}}(t,64k\tilde{d})
; in other words,
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G^{\prime}}(a,b)\leq 256kd<d^{\prime}
, which is impossible. Therefore, the algorithm from
Appendix

A
must return two equal-cardinality sets
A,B
of vertices, with
|A|,|B|\geq\frac{z^{1-1/k}}{3}\geq\frac{|\Pi^{\prime}|}{3n^{1/k}}
, such that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G^{\prime}}(A,B)\geq 2d
.

Since graph
G
is
(d,\eta)
-well-connected with respect to
S
, there is a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
|A|
paths in
G
, connecting vertices of
A
to vertices of
B
, so that the length of every path is at most
d
, and the paths cause congestion at most
\eta
. Since
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G^{\prime}}(A,B)\geq 2d
, every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}
must use an edge of
E^{\prime}
. Therefore,
|E^{\prime}|\geq\frac{|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}\geq\frac{|A|}{\eta}\geq\frac{|\Pi^{\prime}|}{3\eta n^{1/k}}
must hold.
But, as we have established above,

|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{64|\Pi^{\prime}|\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\eta^{\prime}}
. Since
\eta^{\prime}=\left(2^{17}\cdot kdn^{1/k}\log^{2}n\right)\cdot\eta=\left(2^{8}\cdot d^{\prime}n^{1/k}\log^{2}n\right)\cdot\eta
, we get that
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|\Pi^{\prime}|}{4\eta n^{1/k}}
, a contradiction.

Therefore, every matching demand defined over the set
S
of supported vertices must be routable in
G
, and so
G
is a
(\overline{1},d^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
-router with respect to
S
.

Appendix B
Proof of
Section

3

Consider the input graph
G
. Since
|E(G)|\leq\Delta\cdot n^{1+15/k}
, the average vertex degree in
G
is at most
2\Delta\cdot n^{15/k}
. Note that, since
k\leq(\log n)^{1/49}
holds, we get that
k^{45}\leq\frac{\log n}{k^{4}}
, and so:

2^{k^{45}}\leq n^{1/k^{4}}.

(19)

We use the algorithm from
Section

2.8
to compute, in time
O(|E(G)|)\leq O\left(n^{1+O(1/k)}\cdot\Delta\right)\leq O(n^{1+O(\delta)}\cdot\Delta)
, a vertex-split graph
G^{\prime}
for
G
, such that the degrees of all vertices in
G^{\prime}
are at most
4n^{15/k}\cdot\Delta
, and
|V(G^{\prime})|\leq 2|V(G)|
. For convenience, we denote
\hat{n}=|V(G^{\prime})|
, so
n\leq\hat{n}\leq 2n
.
Recall that every edge
e=(u,v)\in E(G)
corresponds to a unique edge
e^{\prime}=(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
of
G^{\prime}
, where
u^{\prime}
is a copy of
u
, and
v^{\prime}
is a copy of
v
; we do not distinguish between these edges. We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

3.2
to the resulting graph
G^{\prime}
, with the same parameters
k,\delta,\Delta^{*}
, and parameter
\left\lceil\Delta\right\rceil
replacing
\Delta
. Notice that
512\leq k\leq(\log n)^{1/49}\leq(\log\hat{n})^{1/49}
,
\frac{1}{k}\leq\delta<\frac{1}{400}
, and
k,\Delta^{*},\left\lceil\Delta\right\rceil
and
\frac{1}{\delta}
are integers, as required.
Moreover,
\hat{n}^{18/k}\leq(2n)^{18/k}\leq n^{19/k}\leq\Delta^{*}
must hold, and, since
\hat{n}^{8/k^{2}}\leq(2n)^{8/k^{2}}\leq n^{9/k^{2}}
, we get that
\Delta^{*}\leq\frac{\Delta}{n^{9/k^{2}}}\leq\frac{\left\lceil\Delta\right\rceil}{\hat{n}^{8/k^{2}}}
holds. Therefore, altogether,
\hat{n}^{18/k}\leq\Delta^{*}\leq\frac{\left\lceil\Delta\right\rceil}{\hat{n}^{8/k^{2}}}\leq\hat{n}
must hold. The maximum vertex degree in
G^{\prime}
is bounded by
4n^{15/k}\cdot\Delta\leq 4\hat{n}^{15/k}\cdot\Delta\leq\hat{n}^{16/k}\cdot\left\lceil\Delta\right\rceil
.
Therefore, graph
G^{\prime}
, with parameters
\hat{n}
,
k,\delta,\Delta^{*}
and
\left\lceil\Delta\right\rceil
is a valid input to the algorithm from
Theorem

3.2
.
Consider the router decomposition
\left({\mathcal{C}},\left\{C^{\prime}\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}\right)
of graph
G^{\prime}
with parameters
\Delta^{*},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta}
and
\rho
, that the algorithm maintains, where

\tilde{d}=k^{19}\cdot 2^{O(1/\delta^{6})}
,

\tilde{\eta}=\hat{n}^{17/k}\leq(2n)^{17/k}\leq n^{19/k}
, and
\rho=\hat{n}^{20/k}\leq(2n)^{20/k}\leq\frac{n^{21/k}}{2}
.

Consider a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and recall that it must be
(\overline{\operatorname{deg}}_{C},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
-router with respect to the set
V(C)
of supported vertices. Let
\hat{C}\subseteq G
be the graph obtained by including all the edges of
C
, and all vertices whose copies lie in
C
. It is then easy to verify that
C
is a vertex-split graph of
\hat{C}
. From
Section

2.8
, graph
\hat{C}
is a
(\overline{\operatorname{deg}}_{\hat{C}},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
-router with respect to the set
V(\hat{C})
of supported vertices. Let
\hat{\mathcal{C}}=\left\{\hat{C}\mid C\in{\mathcal{C}}\right\}
. It is immediate to verify that all clusters in
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
are edge disjoint, and that every edge
e\in E(G)\setminus E^{\operatorname{del}}
lies in some cluster of
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
. It is also immediate to verify that
\sum_{\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}}|V(\hat{C})|\leq\sum_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}|V(C)|\leq\rho\cdot\hat{n}\leq 2\rho n
.

Consider again a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and the corresponding subgraph
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
. We can again define a subgraph
\hat{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\hat{C}
, by including in it all edges of
C^{\prime}
, and all vertices of
G
whose copies lie in
C^{\prime}
. Since graph
C^{\prime}
is a
(\overline{\Delta}^{*},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
-router with respect to the set
V(C^{\prime})
of supported vertices, from
Section

2.8
,
\hat{C}^{\prime}
is a
(\overline{\Delta}^{*},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta})
-router with respect to the set
V(\hat{C}^{\prime})
of supported vertices. Therefore, throughout the algorithm
\left(\hat{\mathcal{C}},\left\{\hat{C}^{\prime}\right\}_{\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}}\right)
is a router decomposition of
G
, for parameters
\Delta^{*},\tilde{d},\tilde{\eta}
and
2\rho\leq n^{21/k}
.

Once the algorithm from
Theorem

3.2
initializes the router decomposition
({\mathcal{C}},\left\{C^{\prime}\right\}_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}})
of
G^{\prime}
, we can initialize the router decomposition
\left(\hat{\mathcal{C}},\left\{\hat{C}^{\prime}\right\}_{\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}}\right)
of
G
within the same asymptotic running time. Recall that the algorithm guaranees that initially,
|E^{\operatorname{del}}|\leq\hat{n}\cdot\left\lceil\Delta\right\rceil\cdot\hat{n}^{2/k^{2}}\leq n^{1+4/k^{2}}\cdot\Delta
.

For all
1\leq i\leq k
, once the algorithm from
Theorem

3.2
processes the batch
\pi_{i}
of edge deletions from
G^{\prime}
and updates the clustering
{\mathcal{C}}
and the subgraphs
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
for
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
accordingly, we update the clustering
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
and the graphs
\hat{C}^{\prime}\subseteq\hat{C}
for
\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}
similarly: whenever an edge is deleted from a cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we delete it from
\hat{C}
; whenever an edge is deleted from or inserted into a graph
C^{\prime}
for some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we insert or delete the same edge from the corresponding graph
\hat{C}^{\prime}
; and whenever a vertex
v
is deleted from some cluster
C
, if
v
is a copy of some vertex
v^{\prime}\in V(G)
, and no other copies of
v^{\prime}
remain in
C
, then we delete
v^{\prime}
from
\hat{C}
and
\hat{C}^{\prime}
as well. It is easy to verify that the time required to update the router decomposition
\left(\hat{\mathcal{C}},\left\{\hat{C}^{\prime}\right\}_{\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}}\right)
of
G
is asymptotically bounded by the time that the algorithm from
Theorem

3.2
spends on processing the batch
\pi_{i}
of updates. Lastly, recall that, following the processing of batch
\pi_{i}
, the number of edges that may be added to
E^{\operatorname{del}}
is bounded by
|\pi_{i}|\cdot\hat{n}^{13/k^{2}}\leq|\pi_{i}|\cdot(2n)^{13/k^{2}}\leq|\pi_{i}|\cdot n^{15/k^{2}}
, as required, and the number of edge insertions and deletions from the graphs in
\left\{\hat{C}^{\prime}\mid\hat{C}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}\right\}
while batch
\pi_{i}
is processed is bounded by the number of edge insertions and deletions from the graphs in
\left\{C^{\prime}\mid C\in{\mathcal{C}}\right\}
, which, in turn, is bounded by
|\pi_{i}|\cdot n^{O(1/k^{2})}
.

Appendix C
Proof of
Section

5

We say that a vertex
v\in V(G)
is scattered if
|B_{G}(v,d)|<n^{1-\epsilon}
. We start with
G^{\prime}=G
, and then delete all isolated vertices from
G^{\prime}
. Over the course of the algorithm, we may delete additional vertices from
G^{\prime}
.

The algorithm consists of a number of iterations. In each iteration, we may delete some vertices from
G^{\prime}
(which must be scattered vertices), and we may mark some additional vertices of
G^{\prime}
as scattered. Throughout, we denote by
U\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
the set of vertices that were not marked as scattered yet. We maintain the following invariants:

I1.

If a vertex
v\in V(G)
is not scattered, then
v\in U
holds at all times; and

I2.

For every vertex
u\in U
,
B_{G}(u,d)\subseteq G^{\prime}
.

The algorithm continues as long as
U\neq\emptyset
and
|V(G^{\prime})|\geq n^{1-\epsilon}
hold. If either of these conditions does not hold, then we are guaranteed that
G
is
(d,\epsilon)
-scattered. Indeed, if
G
contains a vertex
v
with
|B_{G}(v,d)|\geq n^{1-\epsilon}
, then, from Invariant
I1
,
v\in U
, and from Invariant
I2

B_{G}(u,d)\subseteq G^{\prime}
must hold, so
U\neq\emptyset
and
|V(G^{\prime})|\geq n^{1-\epsilon}
must hold. Therefore, if either
U\neq\emptyset
or
|V(G^{\prime})|\geq n^{1-\epsilon}
hold, we terminate the algorithm and report that
G
is
(d,\epsilon)
-scattered.

At the beginning of the algorithm,
U=V(G^{\prime})
, and it is easy to see that both invariants hold. We now describe a single iteration. We assume that both invariants hold at the beginning of the iteration, and we will ensure that they hold at the end of the iteration.

At the beginning of the iteration, we delete from
G^{\prime}
all isolated vertices. It is easy to verify that both invariants continue to hold.
Next, we select an arbitrary vertex
u\in U
. We denote
L_{0}=B_{G^{\prime}}(u,2d)
, and, for all
j>0
, we denote by
L_{j}=B_{G^{\prime}}(u,2(j+1)d)\setminus B_{G^{\prime}}(u,2jd)
. We also denote by
E_{j}
the set of all edges with both endpoints in
L_{0}\cup\cdots\cup L_{j}
.

We perform a BFS in
G^{\prime}
, starting from vertex
u
, until we encounter the first index
j
, for which
|E_{j+1}|\leq m^{\epsilon}\cdot|E_{j}|
. It is easy to verify that
1\leq j\leq 2\left\lfloor 1/\epsilon\right\rfloor
must hold, since otherwise, for all
1\leq j\leq 2\left\lfloor 1/\epsilon\right\rfloor
,
|E_{j}|>m^{\epsilon}\cdot|E_{j-1}|\geq m^{(j-1)\cdot\epsilon}
, and so
|E_{2\left\lfloor 1/\epsilon\right\rfloor}|>m
, which is impossible.

Let
S=L_{0}\cup\cdots\cup L_{j}
, let
S^{\prime\prime}=L_{0}\cup\cdots\cup L_{j+1}
, and let
S^{\prime}=B_{G^{\prime}}(u,2(j+1)d+d)
. In other words,
S^{\prime}
contains all vertices of
S
, and the subsequent
d
layers of the BFS. We now consider two cases. The first case happens if
|S^{\prime\prime}|\geq n^{1-\epsilon}
. In this case, we say the current iteration is good. Notice that
S^{\prime\prime}\subseteq B_{G}(u,4d/\epsilon)
. We then return vertex
u
as the outcome of the algorithm. The running time of the current iteration is bounded by
O(m)
in this case.

Consider now the second case, where
|S^{\prime\prime}|<n^{1-\epsilon}
. In this case, we say that the iteration is bad. Notice that, from our invariants, we are guaranteed that, for every vertex
v\in S^{\prime}\cap U
,
B_{G}(u,d)\subseteq B_{G^{\prime}}(u,d)
, and moreover, since
B_{G^{\prime}}(u,d)\subseteq S^{\prime\prime}
, we get that
|B_{G}(u,d)|\leq n^{1-\epsilon}
. Therefore, every vertex in
S^{\prime}
is scattered. We delete the vertices of
S
from
G
, and we mark the vertices of
S^{\prime}\setminus S
as scattered, so they are deleted from
U
. Notice that, for every vertex
x
that remains in
U
, if
B_{G}(x,d)\subseteq B_{G^{\prime}}(x,d)
held at the beginning of the current iteration, then
B_{G}(x,d)\subseteq B_{G^{\prime}}(x,d)
continues to hold at the end of the current iteration, since
\mbox{\sf dist}_{G^{\prime}}(x,S)>d
. The running time of the current iteration in the second case is bounded by
O(|E_{j+1}|)\leq O(\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(S)\cdot m^{\epsilon})
.

It now remains to analyze the running time of the algorithm. There is at most one good iteration in the algorithm, and its running time is
O(m)
. The running time of a bad iteration is
O(\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(S)\cdot m^{\epsilon})
, where
S
is the set of vertices deleted from
G^{\prime}
in that iteration. Therefore, the total running time of the algorithm is
O(m^{1+\epsilon})
.

Appendix D
Proofs Omitted from
Section

8

D.1
Proof of
Section

8.1.1

Observe first that our algorithm ensures that the graph
C
has no isolated vertices. It also ensures that
C
is a large cluster, so

n^{\prime}=|V(C)|\geq\Delta
holds. From the definition of valid input parameters (see
Section

8
),
n
is greater than a large enough constant, and
k\leq(\log n)^{1/49}
, so
n^{\prime}>\Delta\geq n^{18/k}\geq 2^{18(\log n)^{48/49}}
can also be assumed to be sufficiently large.

Since
n^{\prime}\geq\Delta
, and since, from the definition of valid input graph and parameters,
\Delta\geq 16
, we get that
16\leq\Delta\leq n^{\prime}
holds as required.
From the definition of valid input parameters, we are also guaranteed that
512\leq k\leq(\log n)^{1/49}
holds.
Since
n^{\prime}>\Delta\geq n^{18/k}\geq n^{1/(4k^{2})}
(from the definition of valid input parameters), and
k\leq(\log n)^{1/49}
, we get that:

(\log n^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{4k^{2}}\cdot\log n\geq\frac{\log n}{4(\log n)^{1/20}}\geq(\log n)^{3/5}.

(20)

Since
\hat{k}=k^{2}
, we then get that:

\hat{k}\leq(\log n)^{1/20}\leq(\log n^{\prime})^{1/3}.

Lastly, from the definition of valid input parameters,
1/\delta
must be an integer, with
\frac{1}{k}\leq\delta\leq\frac{1}{400}
. Since
k\leq(\log n)^{1/49}
, we get that
\frac{1}{(\log n)^{1/49}}\leq\frac{1}{k}\leq\delta
, and so
\frac{2}{(\log n^{\prime})^{1/24}}\leq\delta\leq\frac{1}{400}
,
as required.

D.2
Proof of
Section

8.1.1

Recall that the algorithm for the
NiceRouterPruning
problem ensures that the total number of vertices that initially lied in
W_{\hat{k}}^{N,\Delta}
, but no longer lie in
U_{\hat{k}}
at the end of Phase
\Phi_{0}
is bounded by:

\frac{|E^{\prime}_{0}|}{\Delta}\cdot\hat{k}^{4\hat{k}}\leq\frac{N^{\hat{k}}}{2}.

Therefore, at the end of Phase
\Phi_{0}
, graph
W_{C}
and set
U_{\hat{k}}
contain at least
\frac{N^{\hat{k}}}{2}
vertices. From the definition of a properly pruned subgraph (see
Section

4.2
), graph
W_{C}
must contain at least
\frac{|U_{k}|\cdot\Delta}{4}\geq\frac{N^{\hat{k}}\cdot\Delta}{8}
edges.

Since
{\mathcal{P}}
is an embedding of
W_{C}
into
C^{\prime}
, we get that:

|V(C^{\prime})|\geq|V(W_{C})|\\
\geq\frac{N^{\hat{k}}}{2}

holds.
Therefore,
N\leq(2|V(C^{\prime})|)^{1/\hat{k}}
holds. Additionally, since
N=\left\lfloor|V(C)|^{1/\hat{k}-1/\hat{k}^{2}}\right\rfloor
, we get that
N\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot|V(C)|^{1/\hat{k}-1/\hat{k}^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot|V(C^{\prime})|^{1/\hat{k}-1/\hat{k}^{2}}
. Since every edge of
C^{\prime}
lies on some path of
{\mathcal{P}}_{C}
, and every vertex of
C^{\prime}
participates in at least
\frac{\Delta}{n^{4/k^{2}}}
such paths,
we conclude that
\left(W^{N,\Delta}_{\hat{k}},W_{C},{\mathcal{P}}\right)
is a valid router certificate for
C^{\prime}
.

Since the edges of
W_{C}
are embedded into graph
C^{\prime}
with congestion at most
\eta^{*}
, we get that:

|E(C^{\prime})|\geq\frac{|E(W_{C})|}{\eta^{*}}\geq\frac{N^{\hat{k}}\cdot\Delta}{8\eta^{*}}.

Recall that all vertex degrees in graph
G
are bounded by
\Delta\cdot n^{16/k}
, and that
N^{\hat{k}}\geq\frac{|V(C)|}{2^{\hat{k}}\cdot n^{1/\hat{k}}}
. Therefore:

|E(C)|\leq|V(C)|\cdot\Delta\cdot n^{16/k}\leq N^{\hat{k}}\cdot\Delta\cdot n^{17/k}\cdot 2^{k^{2}}.

Altogether, we get that:

|E(C^{\prime})|\geq\frac{N^{\hat{k}}\cdot\Delta}{8\eta^{*}}\geq\frac{|E(C)|}{8\eta^{*}\cdot n^{17/k}\cdot 2^{k^{2}}}\geq\frac{|E(C)|}{n^{18/k}},

since, from Inequalty
15
,
\eta^{*}\leq n^{5/k^{2}}
; we also used Inequality
13
.

D.3
Proof of
Section

8.2

The claim easily follows from the following observation.

Observation D.1
.

There is an algorithm, whose input is a connected bipartite graph
H=(X,Y,E)
, and parameters
z\geq 2
,
\hat{\Delta},\gamma^{\prime}\geq 1
and
r>4\gamma^{\prime}
, such that, for every vertex
x\in X
,
\deg_{H}(x)\geq z\cdot\hat{\Delta}
, and, for every vertex
y\in Y
,
\deg_{H}(y)\leq\gamma^{\prime}\cdot z\cdot\hat{\Delta}
.
The algorithm computes a collection
S\subseteq X
of at least
\frac{|X|}{2}
vertices, and, for every vertex
x\in S
, a collection
E^{\prime}(x)\subseteq\delta_{H}(x)
of its incident edges with
|E^{\prime}(x)|=\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
, such that every vertex
y\in Y
serves as an endpoint in at most
r\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
edges in
\bigcup_{x\in S}E^{\prime}(x)
.
The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E|)
.

We provide the proof of the observation below, after we complete the proof of
Section

8.2
using it.
We let
r=\frac{\hat{r}}{\left\lceil\log|X|\right\rceil}
; notice that

r>4\gamma^{\prime}
must hold.
We start with a graph
H^{\prime}=H
, and perform at most
\left\lceil\log|X|\right\rceil
iterations. In the
i
th iteration, we apply the algorithm from
Section

D.3
to the current graph
H^{\prime}
, with the parameters
\hat{\Delta},z,\gamma^{\prime}
and
r
that remain unchanged. Let
S_{i}\subseteq X
be the collection of vertices computed by the algorithm, and, for every vertex
x\in S_{i}
, let
E^{\prime}(x)
be the correponding subset of its incident edges. We then delete from
H^{\prime}
all vertices of
S_{i}
, and any additional vertices of
Y
that become isolated, and continue to the next iteration. It is immediate to verify that, after at most
\left\lceil\log(|X|)\right\rceil
iteration, graph
H^{\prime}
becomes empty, and, for every vertex
x\in X
, we obtain a collection
E^{\prime}(x)
of its incident edges with
|E^{\prime}(x)|=\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
. Moreover, it is immediate to verify that every vertex
y\in Y
serves as an endpoint in at most
r\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil\cdot\left\lceil\log(|X|)\right\rceil\leq\hat{r}\cdot\hat{\Delta}
edges in
\bigcup_{x\in S}E^{\prime}(x)
. Since the number of iteration is bounded by
O(\log(|X|))
, we get that the running time of the algorithm is
O(|E|\cdot\log(|X|))
.

It now remains to prove
Section

D.3
.

Proof of
Section

D.3
.

Throughout the algorithm, we maintain a subset
S\subseteq X
of vertices, starting with
S=\emptyset
. Whenever a vertex
x
is added to set
S
, we also define a collection
E^{\prime}(x)\subseteq\delta_{H}(x)
of its incident edges with
|E^{\prime}(x)|=\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
. Throughout the algorithm, we denote by
E^{\prime}=\bigcup_{x\in S}E^{\prime}(x)
. For every vertex
y\in Y
, we maintain a counter
n_{y}
, that counts the number of edges in
E^{\prime}
incident to
n_{y}
. We say that vertex
y
is
overloaded
, if
n_{y}\geq(r-1)\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
holds.
At the beginning of the algorithm, we initalize the counter
n_{y}
of every vertex
y\in Y
to
0
.
We then process the vertices of
X
one by one. We now describe the processing of a vertex
x\in X
.

We consider the set
\delta_{H}(x)
of edges incident to
x
in
H
, and we denote by
E^{\prime\prime}(x)\subseteq\delta_{H}(x)
the subset of all such edges, whose endpont
y\in Y
is not overloaded. If
|E^{\prime\prime}(x)|\geq\hat{\Delta}
, then we let
E^{\prime}(x)\subseteq E^{\prime\prime}(x)
be any subset containing
\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
edges. We add
x
to
S
, and, for every vertex
y\in Y
, that serves as an enpoint of at least one edge in
E^{\prime}(x)
, we increase the counter
n_{y}
by the number of edges in
E^{\prime}(x)
for which
y
is an endpoint; notice that, since
|E^{\prime}(x)|=\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
,
n_{y}\leq r\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
continues to hold.
Otherwise, if
|E^{\prime\prime}(x)|<\hat{\Delta}
, then we say that
x
is a
discarded vertex
.
Once all vertices of
X
are processed, we consider the final set
S\subseteq X
, and, for every vertex
x\in S
, the subset
E^{\prime}(x)\subseteq\delta_{H}(x)
of its incident edges. Our algorithm ensures that
|E^{\prime}(x)|=\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
, and that every vertex
y\in Y
serves as an endpoint of at most
r\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
edges in
E^{\prime}=\bigcup_{x\in S}E^{\prime}(x)
. It now only remains to prove that
|S^{\prime}|\geq\frac{|X|}{2}
.

Assume for contradiction that this is not the case, and let
S^{\prime\prime}=X\setminus S^{\prime}
be the set of all discarded vertices, so
|S^{\prime\prime}|\geq\frac{|X|}{2}
. Let
\Pi
be the collection of pairs
(x,y)
, where
x\in S^{\prime\prime}
, and
y\in Y
is an overloaded vertex, that is an endpoint of at least one edge in
\delta_{H}(x)
. From the definition of the discarded vertices, and since the degrees of all vertices in
X
are at least
z\cdot\hat{\Delta}
, we get that
|\Pi|\geq(z-1)\cdot\hat{\Delta}\cdot|S^{\prime\prime}|\geq\frac{z\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil\cdot|X|}{4}
. On the other hand, since every vertex
y\in Y
has degree at most
\hat{\Delta}\cdot z\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
in
H
, we get that every overloaded vertex may participate in at most
\hat{\Delta}\cdot z\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
pairs in
\Pi
. Therefore, at least
\frac{|X|}{4\gamma^{\prime}}
vertices in
Y
are overloaded.

Recall that we have assumed that
|S|\leq\frac{|X|}{2}
, and so
|E^{\prime}|=|S|\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil\leq\frac{|X|\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil}{2}
. If a vertex
y\in Y
is overloaded, then the number of edges in
E^{\prime}
that are incident to
y
is at least
(r-1)\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil
. Therefore, the number of overloaded vertices in
Y
is bounded by:

\frac{|E^{\prime}|}{(r-1)\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil}\leq\frac{2|E^{\prime}|}{r\cdot\left\lceil\hat{\Delta}\right\rceil}\leq\frac{|X|}{r}<\frac{|X|}{4\gamma^{\prime}},

since we have assumed that
r>4\gamma^{\prime}
, a contradiction.
